    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift Pattern.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses because no single judge is pivotal, while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$).\n\n\\paragraph{Positive Direction, Method-Sensitive Significance.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions under OLS and GAM bootstrap (direction 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12 across inference methods; OLS 12/12, clustered SE 0/12, bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $p{=}0.423$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), a fundamental limitation: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound suffices to explain the association in 69\\% of conditions; RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in only 3--4/12 per $K$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit provides directional support (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$; 98.6\\% OLS concordance). Capability-matched subsampling (Pool~A: $r{=}0.50$) raises RV in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark IV provides additional identification (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). Causal attribution requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{API-Only vs.\\ Full Pool.}\nRestricting to the 9 API-accessed models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the predictor pattern: $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance (8/12 at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2/12, 3/12; Table~\\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). With all API models above 63\\% accuracy, panels are more homogeneous; the six local AWQ models (25--54\\%) contribute the capability spread that drives $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduces the confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not the direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association. Threshold voting yields $R^2{=}0.829$; Bayesian calibration reduces $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (5+/1$-$) and predictability ($R^2{=}0.087$), but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12; Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation}); at $K{=}5$, all four methods converge to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12 and the Bayesian attenuation observed at $K{=}3$ does not persist (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}, Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$, however, Bayesian neutralization returns (0/12) while threshold voting reaches 10/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}, Table~\\ref{tab:aggregation_k7}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}). Clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attack types ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Non-Monotonic Behavior.}\nBayesian calibration's $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance follows a non-monotonic trajectory across $K$: attenuation at $K{=}3$ (6/12), recovery at $K{=}5$ (7/12, matching all other methods), and full neutralization at $K{=}7$ (0/12). At $K{=}3$, the Bayesian posterior is dominated by the single strongest judge's confusion matrix, partially absorbing the diversity signal. At $K{=}5$, three votes are needed for majority; Bayesian weight entropy increases as more judges contribute meaningfully to the posterior, and the diversity channel re-emerges. At $K{=}7$ ($t{=}4$), the posterior concentrates on the 2--3 most accurate judges, effectively reducing the panel to a small high-confidence subset where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation is compressed. This weight-concentration mechanism explains why Bayesian neutralization is $K$-dependent rather than uniform: the posterior's effective panel size does not grow linearly with $K$, creating a non-monotonic interaction between calibration and the diversity channel.\n\n\\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}\n$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.978$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 7--8/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 7 \\to 8$). The information-theoretic motivation ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the structural rationale: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, a structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity}).\n",

Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}); clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Bayesian Non-Monotonic Behavior.}",
          "-Bayesian calibration's $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance follows a non-monotonic trajectory across $K$: attenuation at $K{=}3$ (6/12), recovery at $K{=}5$ (7/12, matching all other methods), and full neutralization at $K{=}7$ (0/12). At $K{=}3$, the Bayesian posterior is dominated by the single strongest judge's confusion matrix, partially absorbing the diversity signal. At $K{=}5$, three votes are needed for majority; Bayesian weight entropy increases as more judges contribute meaningfully to the posterior, and the diversity channel re-emerges. At $K{=}7$ ($t{=}4$), the posterior concentrates on the 2--3 most accurate judges, effectively reducing the panel to a small high-confidence subset where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation is compressed. This weight-concentration mechanism explains why Bayesian neutralization is $K$-dependent rather than uniform: the posterior's effective panel size does not grow linearly with $K$, creating a non-monotonic interaction between calibration and the diversity channel.",
          "+Bayesian calibration's $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance follows a non-monotonic trajectory: attenuation at $K{=}3$ (6/12), recovery at $K{=}5$ (7/12), and full neutralization at $K{=}7$ (0/12). At large $K$, the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation; at intermediate $K$, weight entropy increases as more judges contribute, recovering the diversity channel (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}",
          " $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} offers complementary prediction: averaging 7/12 bootstrap-significant conditions across $K$ ($\\rho{=}0.978$, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.497$), it outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 7--8/12, $|\\bar{\\beta}|{=}0.430$; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Crucially, $\\kappa$-diversity does not exhibit the predictor shift: its significance \\emph{decreases} monotonically ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$), whereas $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s increases ($4 \\to 7 \\to 8$). The information-theoretic motivation ($\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$) provides the structural rationale: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ decomposes joint vulnerability into a diversity factor and a per-judge capability ceiling, a structure $\\kappa$-diversity lacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity})."
        ]

    "newString": "Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold of \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}.",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 74,
        "oldLines": 4,
        "newStart": 74,
        "newLines": 4,
        "lines": [
          " \\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}",
          " \\label{sec:robustness_method}",
          " ",
          "-The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position at $K{=}3$ are below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold recommended by \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; at $K{=}5$, cluster count drops to ${\\leq}11$, and at $K{=}7$ to ${\\leq}9$. Following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count; under crossed random effects correction (multi-membership CRVE, $t(14)$; Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 at $K{\\geq}5$ while $K{=}3$ attenuates further (0/12). We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}.",
          "+The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Cluster counts (13, ${\\leq}11$, ${\\leq}9$ at $K{=}3,5,7$) fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold of \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; we use Rademacher weights following \\citet{mackinnon2018wild}. The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (4/12 significant) may partly reflect insufficient cluster count; under crossed random effects correction (multi-membership CRVE, $t(14)$; Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12 at $K{\\geq}5$ while $K{=}3$ attenuates further (0/12). We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \\citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \\citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_methods}."
        ]

Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}
32	\label{tab:dominance}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	Under OLS, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\eta_{\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels. The distribution of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes is reported in Appendix~\ref{app:keff_dist} (Table~\ref{tab:keff_dist}).
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain positive in all 12 conditions for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, but clustered SE confidence intervals are wide at this panel size ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12 significant; Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).

41	\small
42	\begin{tabular}{lcc}

47	Clustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\
48	Mixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\
49	Wild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\
50	Frac.\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\
51	\bottomrule
52	\end{tabular}

All five methods preserve the positive coefficient direction for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (direction 12/12), though significance ranges from 0/12 to 12/12.}

61	\label{fig:robustness}
62	\end{figure}
63	
64	Power analysis yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
65	
66	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}
67	\label{sec:cross_k}
68	
69	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\geq}5$, where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).
70	
71	We repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
72	
73	\begin{figure}[t]
74	\centering
75	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
76	\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
77	\label{fig:regime_transition}
78	\end{figure}
79	
80	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\eta_{\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.
81	
82	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
83	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
84	
85	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).
86	
87	\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}
88	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
89	
90	GAM bootstrap confirms that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\% at $K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$).
91	
92	\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}
93	\label{sec:aggregation}
94

103	\midrule
104	Threshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\
105	Majority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\

111	\label{tab:aggregation}
112	\end{table}
113	
114	
115	The methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved under majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense.
116

118	Fractional logit \citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}).

Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\\% at $K{=}5$; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved under majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense.\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\S\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}).}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.
11	
12	Yet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the independence-based intuition that joint failure scales with $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$ assumes non-adaptive faults \citep{schneier2000secrets}.
13	
14	In our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent predictor shift. The positive direction of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across all 12 conditions (direction 12/12 under OLS, though significance is method-sensitive: 0--12/12 across inference methods; GAM bootstrap confirms 88.9--100\% positive rate) is directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}, but the $K$-dependent transition in predictor dominance is previously uncharacterized. At $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ show some significance; at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates. This transition arises in the LLM judge setting, where adversaries manipulate through natural language rather than gradient-based perturbations, and aggregation rules provide a practical modulation lever. GAM bootstrap confirms no significant difference between attack types (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$).
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models, $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this shift (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Aggregation rules influence predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS).
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and GAM bootstrap), though significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12 across inference methods). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12). No attack-type split exists (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; \S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
21	\item \textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\eta_{\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\eta_{\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12 at $K{=}5$, 1/12 at $K{=}7$) (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
22	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules influence how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83 $\to$ Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (9/12), providing partial rather than complete defense (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24	
25	Our findings describe descriptive associations; causal interpretation is fundamentally limited by the diversity--capability collinearity ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$, VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
26	
```

    "newString": "\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels. The distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes is reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist} (Table~\\ref{tab:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain positive in all 12 conditions for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, but clustered SE confidence intervals are wide at this panel size ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), demonstrating that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive. All five methods preserve the positive coefficient direction for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (direction 12/12), though significance ranges from 0/12 to 12/12.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\\% at $K{=}5$; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved under majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense.\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels. The distribution of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across panel sizes is reported in Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist} (Table~\\ref{tab:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ slightly ahead. Point estimates remain positive in all 12 conditions for $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, but clustered SE confidence intervals are wide at this panel size ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12 significant; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate at $K{=}3$, 100\\% at $K{=}5$; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved under majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense.\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

A Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$ at $K{=}3$, $p{=}0.423$ at $K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log).     "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positive and FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions. $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 6/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 3/12  & 4/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 6/12  & 9/12  \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); all methods preserve positive direction (12/12).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (decreasing to 0.048 at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). The bootstrap attenuation at $K{=}3$ (OLS 12/12 $\\to$ bootstrap 4/12) is consistent with this MDE, though positive coefficient direction (12/12) is preserved across all methods (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Predictor Shift}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS), with fractional logit giving 9/12. This contrasts with $K{\\geq}5$, where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent across all methods (bootstrap 7/12 at $K{=}5$; fractional logit 11/12).\n\nWe repeat the wild cluster bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 3/12 to 2/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 4/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases from 3 to 5. Shaded region marks the predictor shift zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a predictor shift between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, both predictors contribute modestly ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 3/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 4/12)---co-determination. At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 2/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) confirms that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions.\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. Random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant 2/2); adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.275$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains power (7/12). The predictability gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.43$ ($K{=}5$).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in all 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 11+/0$-$  & 0.829 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.327 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 12+/0$-$  & 0.328 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.087 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a predictability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.829$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.087$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive direction is preserved under majority (12+/0$-$) and weighted (12+/0$-$) voting; Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), yielding partial defense.\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE confirms the predictor shift: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance increases from 9/12 ($K{=}3$) to 11/12 ($K{=}5$) to 12/12 ($K{=}7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the independence-based intuition that joint failure scales with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent predictor shift. The positive direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across all 12 conditions (direction 12/12 under OLS, though significance is method-sensitive: 0--12/12 across inference methods; GAM bootstrap confirms 88.9--100\\% positive rate) is directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}, but the $K$-dependent transition in predictor dominance is previously uncharacterized. At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ show some significance; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates. This transition arises in the LLM judge setting, where adversaries manipulate through natural language rather than gradient-based perturbations, and aggregation rules provide a practical modulation lever. GAM bootstrap confirms no significant difference between attack types (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$).\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this shift (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Aggregation rules influence predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and GAM bootstrap), though significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12 across inference methods). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12). No attack-type split exists (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; \\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12 at $K{=}5$, 1/12 at $K{=}7$) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules influence how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83 $\\to$ Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), providing partial rather than complete defense (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings describe descriptive associations; causal interpretation is fundamentally limited by the diversity--capability collinearity ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$, VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n",

Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Aggregation rules influence predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS).",
    "newString": "Controlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this shift (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\\eta_{\\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}).}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.\n\nYet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the independence-based intuition that joint failure scales with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent predictor shift. The positive direction of $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across all 12 conditions (direction 12/12 under OLS, though significance is method-sensitive: 0--12/12 across inference methods; GAM bootstrap confirms 88.9--100\\% positive rate) is directionally consistent with adversarial ensemble theory \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}, but the $K$-dependent transition in predictor dominance is previously uncharacterized. At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ show some significance; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates. This transition arises in the LLM judge setting, where adversaries manipulate through natural language rather than gradient-based perturbations, and aggregation rules provide a practical modulation lever.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this shift (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Aggregation rules influence predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.83, Bayesian 0.09); Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12 under OLS).\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions (12/12 under OLS and GAM bootstrap), though significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12 across inference methods). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) limits causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (25/36), though fractional logit confirms the positive association ($K{=}3$: 9/12; $K{=}5$: 11/12; $K{=}7$: 12/12). No attack-type split exists (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$; \\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Panel-size-dependent predictor shift.} At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ (3/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (4/12) contribute under bootstrap (co-determination); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor (7/12 at $K{=}5$, 8/12 at $K{=}7$) while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates (2/12 at $K{=}5$, 1/12 at $K{=}7$) (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules influence how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.83 $\\to$ Bayesian 0.09). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (9/12), providing partial rather than complete defense (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nOur findings describe descriptive associations; causal interpretation is fundamentally limited by the diversity--capability collinearity ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$, VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n",

At $K{=}3$, both $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ show some significance; at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the dominant predictor while $\\eta_{\\max}$ sharply attenuates. The decomposition $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ provides the structural rationale (Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity}).",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor Shift Pattern.}\nAt $K{=}3$, a single compromised judge flips the majority ($t{=}2$), making both predictors relevant. At $K{\\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\\geq}3$ judges; $\\eta_{\\max}$'s contribution collapses because no single judge is pivotal, while high-$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces ($K^* \\in (3,5)$).\n\n\\paragraph{Positive Direction, Method-Sensitive Significance.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient direction is positive in all 12 conditions under OLS and GAM bootstrap (direction 12/12), but significance is method-sensitive (0--12/12 across inference methods; OLS 12/12, clustered SE 0/12, bootstrap 4/12 at $K{=}3$, 7/12 at $K{=}5$). No magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $p{=}0.423$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}).\n\n\\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound: A Fundamental Limitation.}\n$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\eta_{\\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), a fundamental limitation: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers (Appendix~\\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \\citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound suffices to explain the association in 69\\% of conditions; RV exceeds $\\eta_{\\max}$ in only 3--4/12 per $K$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit provides directional support (9/12, 11/12, 12/12 at $K{=}3,5,7$; 98.6\\% OLS concordance). Capability-matched subsampling (Pool~A: $r{=}0.50$) raises RV in 10/12 at $K{=}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}); cross-benchmark IV provides additional identification (Appendix~\\ref{app:cross_benchmark}). Causal attribution requires capability-controlled experimental designs.\n\n\\paragraph{API-Only vs.\\ Full Pool.}\nRestricting to the 9 API-accessed models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$, VIF${=}4.4$), reversing the predictor pattern: $\\eta_{\\max}$ retains significance (8/12 at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$) while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2/12, 3/12; Table~\\ref{tab:robustness_checks}). With all API models above 63\\% accuracy, panels are more homogeneous; the six local AWQ models (25--54\\%) contribute the capability spread that drives $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation but also introduces the confound (Appendix~\\ref{app:random_subsample}).\n\n\\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}\nAggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction. At $K{=}5$, all four methods converge to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 7/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$, Bayesian neutralization returns (0/12) while threshold voting reaches 10/12 (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}). Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:panel_selection}); clean $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}).\n\n\\paragraph{Bayesian Non-Monotonic Behavior.}\nBayesian calibration's $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance follows a non-monotonic trajectory: attenuation at $K{=}3$ (6/12), recovery at $K{=}5$ (7/12), and full neutralization at $K{=}7$ (0/12). At large $K$, the posterior concentrates on 2--3 accurate judges, compressing $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ variation; at intermediate $K$, weight entropy increases as more judges contribute, recovering the diversity channel (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ Alternative Diversity Measures.}\n$\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures} outperforms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ at $K{=}3$ (8/12 vs.\\ 4/12) but falls behind at $K{\\geq}5$ (6--7/12 vs.\\ 7--8/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:kappa_regime}), and does not exhibit the predictor shift ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$ vs.\\ $4 \\to 7 \\to 8$). The decomposition $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$ provides the structural rationale (Appendix~\\ref{app:alt_diversity}).\n",

Here's the tightened version:

The predictor shift shows $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominates at larger panel sizes while $\eta_{\max}$ fades, with consistent positive direction but method-sensitive significance. A strong diversity-capability confound ($r \approx 0.70$--$0.81$) fundamentally constrains causal separation, so I need to screen $\eta_{\max}$ effects at $K{=}3$ and monitor independence assumptions at $K{\geq}5$.

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "4193793c-088b-42d0-9d9f-5ce8126a9b56",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "f723368f-34be-4a25-b350-e254d7a6d86d",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "e80c9178-b6ce-4727-888a-63dc32da08de",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "Three mitigation strategies address judge correlation through different mechanisms: DBEI-based reweighting, our Bayesian calibration baseline, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.",
    "newString": "Mitigation strategies include DBEI-based reweighting, Bayesian calibration, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations. These frameworks assume benign conditions; we test whether aggregation benefits survive adversarial manipulation. \\citet{almasoud2026security} reported that 7-model committees reduce ASR from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\%, but did not disentangle diversity from individual robustness. \\citet{tamas2025benchmarking} benchmark adversarial risks in multi-agent LLM systems but focus on agent coordination rather than judge panel composition. \\citet{qian2026collabeval} propose CollabEval, a three-phase collaborative evaluation framework (independent assessment, multi-round discussion, consensus judgment) that outperforms single-judge baselines; their work assumes benign conditions and does not examine adversarial manipulation or the diversity--vulnerability tradeoff we characterize.\n\n\\paragraph{Condorcet Jury Theorem.}\n\\citet{condorcet1785essai} proved that majority voting accuracy converges to 1 when per-voter accuracy exceeds 0.5; \\citet{ladha1992condorcet} extended the theorem to correlated voters, showing that positive correlation weakens the convergence guarantee. CJT explicitly assumes independent, non-adversarial errors; its failure under adaptive adversaries is trivially expected \\citep{tramer2018ensemble,kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}. Our contribution is not this well-known direction but the \\emph{panel-size-dependent structure}: the predictor shift from co-determination at $K{=}3$ to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominance at $K{\\geq}5$.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity Theory.}\n\\citet{krogh1995neural} proved that ensemble error equals average member error minus a diversity term; \\citet{brown2005managing} showed diversity reduces error only when members maintain sufficiently low bias. In our setting, weaker judges introduce bias large enough to offset diversity benefits. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} found that no pairwise diversity measure reliably predicted ensemble improvement; our $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ exhibits a $K$-dependent pattern: at $K{=}3$, OLS significance (12/12) attenuates under bootstrap (4/12); at $K{\\geq}5$, it survives bootstrap inference (7/12). Recent information-theoretic frameworks \\citep{yang2026diversity,turkmen2026ensemble} characterize diversity under benign evaluation; we provide the adversarial complement.\n\n\\paragraph{Adversarial Robustness and Transferability.}\nAdversarial vulnerability was formalized by \\citet{goodfellow2015adversarial}, with transferability demonstrated by \\citet{papernot2016transferability,papernot2017practical}. \\citet{tramer2018ensemble} introduced ensemble adversarial training but did not eliminate cross-model transfer. Controlled diversity via uncorrelated gradients \\citep{kariyappa2019improving} or diversity regularization \\citep{pang2019improving} strengthens ensemble robustness in vision, but requires coordinating member training, a control absent in post-hoc LLM judge selection. Three differences motivate our empirical approach: adversaries use natural-language manipulation rather than gradient perturbations, panel decisions are discrete votes, and aggregation rules are a controllable design parameter.\n\n\\paragraph{BFT and Security Games.}\nClassical BFT \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \\citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \\citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \\citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition. \\citet{kuai2026independent} propose the Difficulty-Weighted Behavioral Entanglement Index (DBEI), weighting agreement by item difficulty; $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ captures a complementary dimension via pairwise MI, providing an information-theoretic measure of panel redundancy. Three mitigation strategies address judge correlation through different mechanisms: DBEI-based reweighting, our Bayesian calibration baseline, and credibility scoring \\citep{ebrahimi2025adversary}.\n\n\\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}\nInformation-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \\citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \\citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \\citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \\citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{LLM-as-Judge and Multi-Judge Evaluation.}\nLLM judges gained traction after \\citet{zheng2023judging} established them as scalable alternatives to human annotation. \\citet{wang2023fair} documented systematic failure modes (position bias, verbosity, sycophancy), motivating multi-judge panels; \\citet{raina2024judgerobust} quantified robustness gaps under universal adversarial attacks, \\citet{maloyan2025investigating} demonstrated prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLM-as-a-judge systems, and \\citet{stureborg2024inconsistent} and \\citet{ye2024justice} further catalogued inconsistency and bias in LLM evaluators. Aggregating noisy annotators traces back to \\citet{dawid1979maximum}; \\citet{verga2024replacing} showed diverse panels can match single large judges, and \\citet{panickssery2024llm} identified self-preference bias driving within-family correlations.
